I can... get my point across fine. When I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel confused about where you are or what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: No... I don't have that problem. I always know where I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disoriented or unsure about the date or time?\n\nPatient: No. I know what day it is. I keep track of the time just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty remembering how to do things you've learned to do in the past, like hobbies or work-related skills?\n\nPatient: No... I still know how to do things. That hasn't been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently, like what you did yesterday or last week?\n\nPatient: No, I remember things fine. Yesterday... last week, it's all there. Memory isn't the issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble learning new information?\n\nPatient: No, I can learn things alright. It's just... I don't really sit still long enough for much of that, but the learning part is fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness is because you're not paying attention when people tell you things or when you're supposed to do them?\n\nPatient: I suppose so. It's not that I don't try to listen. I'll sit down at the bridge table, or... a Fix-It Friends planning meeting, meaning to pay close attention. But after a little while, my mind just sort of slides off. Then I realize I missed half of what was said. So... yeah, probably.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind slides off because you're distracted by something else or because you're just having trouble staying focused?\n\nPatient: I guess it's distractions. It doesn't take much. If somebody coughs... or a chair scrapes... I lose my place. Even just seeing someone walk past the window at the senior center. I'm off looking at that instead of the repair list, or... the card game.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would remember things better if you were in a quieter, less distracting environment?\n\nPatient: I don't know. Maybe. I haven't really thought about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like... if I sit down at the bridge table, or a Fix-It Friends planning meeting. \n\nI mean to pay close attention, but... after a little while, my mind just sort of slides off. I'll realize I missed half of what was said. So... yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts in a coherent and logical way?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can... get my point across fine. When I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel confused about where you are or what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: No... I don't have that problem. I always know where I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disoriented or unsure about the date or time?\n\nPatient: No. I know what day it is. I keep track of the time just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty remembering how to do things you've learned to do in the past, like hobbies or work-related skills?\n\nPatient: No... I still know how to do things. That hasn't been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently, like what you did yesterday or last week?\n\nPatient: No, I remember things fine. I don't really sit still long enough for much of that, but the learning part is fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness is because you're not paying attention when people tell you things or when you're supposed to do them?\n\nPatient: I suppose so. It's not that I don't try to listen. I'll sit down at the bridge table, or... a Fix-It Friends planning meeting, meaning to pay close attention. But after a little while, my mind just sort of slides off. Then I realize I missed half of what was said. It doesn't take much. If somebody coughs... or a chair scrapes... I lose my place. Even just seeing someone walk past the window at the senior center. I'm off looking at that instead of the repair list, or... the card game.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would remember things better if you were in a quieter, less distracting environment?\n\nPatient: I don't know. Maybe. I haven't really thought about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to remember things if someone reminded you or helped you stay on track?\n\nPatient: Hard to say. I don't really have anyone... checking up on me like that to keep me on track. So I couldn't tell you if it would work or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would remember instructions or tasks if you wrote them down?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. I guess I could try that. I don't really carry a notepad around, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would remember things better if someone directly told them to you, rather than you having to pay attention to them in a conversation?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I suppose... maybe? It's hard to say if that would really make much of a difference. I haven't really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to recall the information if someone repeated it back to you immediately after you forgot it?\n\nPatient: Could be. I mean... if they just said it again right then, maybe it would stick. But I really couldn't say for sure. I haven't paid much attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would remember things if you were able to focus your attention on them without any distractions?\n\nPatient: Hard to say. I'm off looking at that instead of the repair list, or... the card game.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would remember things better if you were in a quieter, less distracting environment?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I haven't really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to recall the information if someone repeated it back to you immediately after you forgot it?\n\nPatient: Could be. I mean... if they just said it again right then, maybe it would stick. But I really couldn't say for sure. I guess... maybe. But there's always something going on around me, so I don't really know. I suppose... if I was focused right then, maybe. But I just haven't thought about it like that.
